# Alternative for Germany

"AfD" redirects here. For other uses, see [AFD (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AFD_\(disambiguation\)).

Not to be confused with [Alliance for Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alliance_for_Germany).

**Alternative for Germany** ([German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_language): *Alternative für Deutschland*, **AfD** [\[aːʔɛfˈdeː\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Standard_German) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/d/d3/De-AfD.ogg/De-AfD.ogg.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/d/d3/De-AfD.ogg/De-AfD.ogg.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup>) is a [far-right](18526492.md),[^cite_note-farright-20] [right-wing populist](8354382.md),[^cite_note-rwpopulist-21] [national conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_conservatism),[^cite_note-22][^cite_note-23] and in parts [*völkisch* nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V%C3%B6lkisch_nationalism)[^cite_note-24] political party in Germany. It has 151 [members of the Bundestag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Member_of_the_Bundestag) and 15 [members of the European Parliament](258909.md). It is the largest opposition party in the [Bundestag](3768.md) and a member of the [Europe of Sovereign Nations Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europe_of_Sovereign_Nations_Group) in the [European Parliament](9581.md).[^cite_note-25]

Its name reflects its resistance to the mainstream policies of [Angela Merkel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angela_Merkel) and her slogan *Alternativlosigkeit* (lit. 'alternative-less-ness', a German version of "[there is no alternative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/There_is_no_alternative)").[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerbuirLewandowskySiri2015162–163-26] Established in April 2013, AfD narrowly missed the 5% [electoral threshold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_threshold) to sit in the [Bundestag](3768.md) during the [2013 federal election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_German_federal_election). The party won seven seats in the [2014 European Parliament election in Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_European_Parliament_election_in_Germany) as a member of the [European Conservatives and Reformists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Conservatives_and_Reformists_Group) (ECR). After securing representation in 14 of the 16 [German state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_state) parliaments by October 2017, AfD won 94 seats in the [2017 federal election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_German_federal_election) and became the third-largest party in the country, as well as the largest opposition party; its lead candidates were the co-vice chairman [Alexander Gauland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Gauland) and [Alice Weidel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alice_Weidel), the latter having served as the party group leader in the [19th Bundestag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/19th_Bundestag). In the [2021 federal election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_German_federal_election), AfD dropped to being the fifth-largest party in the [20th Bundestag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_members_of_the_20th_Bundestag).[^cite_note-Schultheis_2021-27] Following the [2025 federal election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_German_federal_election), it became the second-largest party and the largest opposition party in the [21st Bundestag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/21st_Bundestag).

AfD was founded by Gauland, [Bernd Lucke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernd_Lucke), and former members of the [Christian Democratic Union of Germany](60584.md) (CDU) to oppose the policies of the [eurozone](184391.md) as a right-wing and moderately [Eurosceptic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euroscepticism_in_Germany) alternative to the [centre-right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre-right) but [pro-European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro-European) CDU. The party presented itself as an [economically liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalism),[^cite_note-Economic_liberal-28] [Eurosceptic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurosceptic), and [conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative) movement in its early years.[^cite_note-Kessel2015-29][^cite_note-Thompson2014-30][^cite_note-McGowanPhinnemore2015-31] AfD subsequently moved further to the right,[^cite_note-bbc.com-32] and expanded its policies under successive leaderships to include opposition to immigration,[^cite_note-timesofisraelantiislam-33][^cite_note-Antiislam1-34] [Islam](6037917.md),[^cite_note-Antiislam2-35] and the [European Union](9317.md).[^cite_note-36] Since 2015, with [the beginning](46415102.md) of the [refugee crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refugee_crisis) in Europe, AfD's ideology has been characterised by [German nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_nationalist),[^cite_note-NYT2017-01-37][^cite_note-Duke-38][^cite_note-Spiegel2016-03-39] *völkisch* nationalism ([ethno-nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethno-nationalism)),[^cite_note-LeserSpissinger2023-40] right-wing populism,[^cite_note-rwpopulism-41] and national conservatism.[^cite_note-Franzmann2015-42][^cite_note-D'OttavioSaalfeld2015-43][^cite_note-HeinischMassetti2020-44] It has a policy focus on [opposing Islam](2394308.md),[^cite_note-thousands_rally-45][^cite_note-Antiislam3-46][^cite_note-theatlantic2-47] [opposing immigration](6491626.md) [into Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_Germany),[^cite_note-48] especially [Muslim immigration into Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Germany),[^cite_note-49] [welfare chauvinism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_chauvinism),[^cite_note-LeserSpissinger2023-40] Euroscepticism,[^cite_note-Arzheimer_2015-50][^cite_note-Eurosceptic-51] [denial of human-caused global warming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denial_of_human-caused_climate_change),[^cite_note-52][^cite_note-53] and [supporting closer relations with Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AfD_pro-Russia_movement).[^cite_note-\:7-54]

Several state associations and other factions of AfD have been linked to or accused of harbouring connections with far-right nationalist and proscribed movements, such as [Pegida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pegida), the *[Neue Rechte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neue_Rechte)*, and the [Identitarian movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Identitarian_movement),[^cite_note-Bennhold030321-55] and of employing [historical revisionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_revisionism),[^cite_note-56] as well as [xenophobic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xenophobic) rhetoric.[^cite_note-57][^cite_note-58][^cite_note-59] They have been observed by various [state offices for the protection of the constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_offices_for_the_protection_of_the_constitution) since 2018.[^cite_note-Spiegel1-60] AfD's leadership has denied that the party is racist and has been internally divided on whether to endorse such groups.[^cite_note-61] In January 2022, after a failed power struggle, party leader [Jörg Meuthen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%B6rg_Meuthen) resigned his party chairmanship with immediate effect and left AfD, stating that the party had moved far to the right with totalitarian traits and in large parts was no longer based on Germany's [liberal democratic basic order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_democratic_basic_order).[^cite_note-Meuthen1-62][^cite_note-Meuthen2-63] Lucke had left the party in 2015 with a similar view.[^cite_note-\:8-64] In 2023, the [Young Alternative for Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Young_Alternative_for_Germany) was officially categorized by the [Federal Office for the Protection of the Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Office_for_the_Protection_of_the_Constitution) (BfV) as a confirmed extremist organisation.[^cite_note-ywtoi-65] In 2025, the BfV officially classified AfD as a "confirmed right-wing extremist endeavor",[^cite_note-\:10-66] which allows authorities to increase surveillance, and permits the use of informants and monitoring of communications,[^cite_note-provenextremist-67] though this classification was suspended following a 2026 court injunction.[^cite_note-u975-68]

AfD is strongest in the areas of the former communist German Democratic Republic ([East Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Germany)), especially the states of [Saxony](28395.md) and [Thuringia](31130.md), largely due to economic and integration issues that continue to persist post-[reunification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_reunification),[^cite_note-69][^cite_note-70][^cite_note-71] in addition to the East German voters' apparent propensity for [strongman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_strongman) rule.[^cite_note-72]

## History

### Background

In September 2012, [Alexander Gauland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Gauland), [Bernd Lucke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernd_Lucke), and the journalist [Konrad Adam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konrad_Adam) founded the political group Electoral Alternative 2013 ([German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_language): *Wahlalternative 2013*) in [Bad Nauheim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bad_Nauheim), to oppose German federal policies concerning the [eurozone crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurozone_crisis), and to confront German-supported bailouts for poorer southern European countries.[^cite_note-73] Their [manifesto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manifesto) was endorsed by several economists, journalists, and business leaders, and stated that the [eurozone](184391.md) had proven to be "[unsuitable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Optimum_currency_area)" as a currency area and that southern European states were "sinking into poverty under the competitive pressure of the euro".[^cite_note-Welt0303-74]

Some candidates of what would become AfD sought election in [Lower Saxony](18435.md) as part of the *Electoral Alternative 2013* in alliance with the [Free Voters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Voters), an association participating in local elections without specific federal or foreign policies, and received 1% of the vote.[^cite_note-Welt0303-74][^cite_note-75] In February 2013, the group decided to found a new party to compete in the 2013 federal election; according to a leaked email from Lucke, the Free Voters leadership declined to join forces.[^cite_note-76]

### Founding



Konrad Adam (left), Frauke Petry, and Bernd Lucke during the first AfD convention on 14 April 2013 in Berlin

The party was founded on 6 February 2013. On 14 April 2013, the AfD announced its presence to the wider public when it held its first convention in [Berlin](3354.md), elected the party leadership, and adopted a party platform. Bernd Lucke,[^cite_note-DailyPolitics-77] the entrepreneur [Frauke Petry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frauke_Petry) and Konrad Adam were elected as speakers.[^cite_note-78] AfD's federal board also chose Alexander Gauland, Roland Klaus, and Patricia Casale as its three deputy speakers. The party elected the treasurer Norbert Stenzel and the three assessors Irina Smirnova, Beatrix Diefenbach, and Wolf-Joachim Schünemann. The economist [Joachim Starbatty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joachim_Starbatty), along with [Jörn Kruse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%B6rn_Kruse?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Helga Luckenbach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helga_Luckenbach?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Dirk Meyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dirk_Meyer_\(economist\)?action=edit\&redlink=1), and [Roland Vaubel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roland_Vaubel?action=edit\&redlink=1), were elected to the party's scientific advisory board. Between 31 March and 12 May 2013 AfD founded affiliates in all 16 [states of Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/States_of_Germany) in order to participate in the federal elections. On 15 June 2013 the [Young Alternative for Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Young_Alternative_for_Germany) was founded in [Darmstadt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darmstadt) as the AfD's youth organisation.[^cite_note-Bernd_Lucke_und_die_wilde_Jugend-79] During the British prime minister [David Cameron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Cameron)'s visit to Germany in April 2013, the British [Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(UK\)) was reported to have contacted both AfD and the [Free Voters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Voters) to discuss possible cooperation, supported by the [European Conservatives and Reformists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Conservatives_and_Reformists_Group) (ECR) group of the European Parliament.[^cite_note-80] In June 2013 Bernd Lucke gave a question and answer session organized by the Conservative Party-allied [Bruges Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruges_Group_\(United_Kingdom\)) [think tank](37101.md) in [Portcullis House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portcullis_House), London.[^cite_note-81] In a detailed report in the conservative *[Frankfurter Allgemeine Zeitung](513356.md)* in April 2013, the paper's Berlin-based political correspondent Majid Sattar revealed that the [Social Democratic Party of Germany](60585.md) (SPD) and CDU had conducted [opposition research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opposition_research) to blunt the growth and attraction of AfD.[^cite_note-Weinthal-82]

Advocating the abolition of the euro, AfD took a more radical stance than the Free Voters.[^cite_note-Pop-83] The [Pirate Party Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pirate_Party_Germany) opposed any coalition with AfD at their 2013 spring convention.[^cite_note-84] The AfD's initial supporters were the same prominent economists, business leaders, and journalists who had supported the Electoral Alternative 2013, including former members of the [Christian Democratic Union of Germany](60584.md) (CDU), who had previously challenged the constitutionality of the German government's eurozone policies at the [Federal Constitutional Court](34781059.md).[^cite_note-85][^cite_note-86][^cite_note-BusinessInsider-87] AfD did not regard itself as a splinter party from the CDU, as its early membership also contained a former state leader from the [Free Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Democratic_Party_\(Germany\)) and members of the Federation of Independent Voters, a pressure group of independents and small business owners.[^cite_note-Arzheimer_2015-50]

### Lucke's leadership (2013–2015)

Further information: [2013 German federal election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_German_federal_election) and [2014 European Parliament election in Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_European_Parliament_election_in_Germany)



Second vote share percentage for AfD in the 2013 federal election in Germany, final results

On 22 September 2013 AfD won 4.7% of the votes in the 2013 federal election, just missing the [5% barrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_system_of_Germany#Electoral_threshold) to enter the [Bundestag](3768.md). The party won about 2 million party list votes and 810,000 constituency votes, which was 1.9% of the total of these votes cast across Germany.[^cite_note-88]

AfD did not participate in the [2013 Bavaria state election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Bavaria_state_election) held on 15 September. The party gained parliamentary representation for the first time in the state parliament of [Hesse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hesse), with the defection of Jochen Paulus from the [Free Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Democratic_Party_\(Germany\)) to AfD in early May 2013;[^cite_note-Paulick-89] he was not re-elected and left office in January 2014.[^cite_note-90] In the [2013 Hesse state election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Hesse_state_election) held on 22 September, the same day as the 2013 federal election, AfD failed to gain representation with just 4% of the vote.



Former "Courage \[to stand up] for the truth! The euro is dividing Europe!" tagline on election placard 2013

In early 2014, the [Federal Constitutional Court of Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Constitutional_Court_of_Germany) ruled the proposed 3% vote hurdle for representation in the European elections unconstitutional, and the 2014 European Parliament election became the first run in Germany without a barrier for representation.[^cite_note-91]

AfD held a party conference on 25 January 2014 in [Aschaffenburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aschaffenburg), northwest [Bavaria](3764.md). The conference chose the slogan *Mut zu Deutschland* ("Courage \[to stand up] for Germany") to replace the former slogan *Mut zur Wahrheit* (lit. 'Courage \[to speak] the truth', or more succinctly, "Telling it as it is"),[^cite_note-Benzow-92] which prompted disagreement among the federal board that the party could be seen as too anti-European. A compromise was reached by using the slogan "MUT ZU D\*EU\*TSCHLAND", with the "EU" in "DEUTSCHLAND" encircled by the 12 stars of the [European flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_flag).[^cite_note-Lachmann-93] The conference elected the top six candidates for the European elections on 26 January 2014 and met again the following weekend to choose the remaining euro candidates.[^cite_note-Benzow-92][^cite_note-Lachmann-93][^cite_note-94] Candidates from 7th–28th place on the party list were selected in Berlin on 1 February.[^cite_note-95] Party chairman Bernd Lucke was elected as lead candidate.

In February 2014, AfD officials said they had discussed alliances with the British anti-EU [UK Independence Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UK_Independence_Party) (UKIP), which Lucke and the federal board of AfD opposed, and also with the [European Conservatives and Reformists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Conservatives_and_Reformists_Group) (ECR) group, to which Britain's [Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(Britain\)) belongs.[^cite_note-Marsh-96] In April 2014 [Hans-Olaf Henkel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hans-Olaf_Henkel), AfD's second candidate on the European election list, ruled out forming a group with UKIP.[^cite_note-WaterfieldII-97] stating that he saw the Conservatives as the preferred partner in the European Parliament.[^cite_note-WaterfieldII-97] On 10 May 2014 Lucke had been in talks with the Czech and Polish member parties of the ECR group.[^cite_note-Barker11May-98]



AfD election poster from 2014. The slogan translates as "Washington spies. Brussels dictates. Berlin obeys."

In the 2014 European Parliament election on 25 May, AfD came in fifth place in Germany, with 7.1% of the national vote (2,065,162 votes), and seven [Members of the European parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Members_of_the_European_Parliament) (MEPs).[^cite_note-Bu-99] On 12 June 2014, it was announced that AfD had been accepted into the ECR group in the European Parliament.[^cite_note-Nicolaou-100] The official vote result was not released to the public, but figures of 29 votes for and 26 against were reported by the membership.[^cite_note-Nicolaou-100] The inclusion of AfD in the ECR group was said to have caused mild tensions between the German chancellor [Angela Merkel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angela_Merkel) and the British prime minister [David Cameron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Cameron).[^cite_note-Arzheimer_2015-50]

On 31 August AfD received 9.7% of the vote in the [2014 Saxony state election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Saxony_state_election),[^cite_note-101] winning 14 seats in the [Landtag of Saxony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landtag_of_the_Free_State_of_Saxony).[^cite_note-102] On 14 September, AfD obtained 10.6% of the vote in the [2014 Thuringian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Thuringian_state_election) and 12.2% in the [Brandenburg state election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Brandenburg_state_election), winning 11 seats in both state parliaments.[^cite_note-103]

On 15 February 2015 AfD won 6.1% of the vote in the [Hamburg state election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Hamburg_state_election), gaining the mandate for eight seats in the [Hamburg Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamburg_Parliament),[^cite_note-104] winning their first seats in a western German state. On 10 May 2015, AfD secured in the 5.5% of the vote in the [2015 Bremen state election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Bremen_state_election) gaining representation in their fifth state parliament on a 50% turnout.[^cite_note-105]

### Petry's leadership (2015–2017)

After months of factional infighting and a cancelled party gathering in June 2015, [Frauke Petry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frauke_Petry) was elected on 4 July 2015 as the *[de facto](43594.md)* principal speaker of the party with 60% of the member votes ahead of Bernd Lucke at a party congress in [Essen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Essen).[^cite_note-106] Petry was a member of the national-conservative faction of AfD.[^cite_note-107] Her leadership was widely seen as heralding a shift of the party to the right to focus more on issues such as immigration, Islam, and strengthening ties to [Russia](25391.md), a shift which was claimed by Lucke as turning the party into a "[Pegida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pegida) party".[^cite_note-108] In the following week, five MEPs exited the party on 7 July, the only remaining MEPs being [Beatrix von Storch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beatrix_von_Storch) and [Marcus Pretzell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcus_Pretzell),[^cite_note-109] and Lucke announced on 8 July 2015 that he was resigning from AfD, citing the rise of xenophobic and pro-Russian sentiments in the party.[^cite_note-110] At a meeting of members of the Wake-up call (Weckruf 2015) group on 19 July 2015, AfD founder Bernd Lucke and former AfD members announced they would form a new party, the [Alliance for Progress and Renewal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Conservative_Reformers), under the founding principles of AfD.[^cite_note-DW_ALFA_founding-111][^cite_note-BBC_ALFA_founding-112] The split off party was eventually renamed the [Liberal Conservative Reformers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Conservative_Reformers), but had little electoral success.[^cite_note-FAZ_ALFA_renaming-113][^cite_note-114]

In February 2016 AfD announced a cooperation pact with the [Freedom Party of Austria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Party_of_Austria) (FPÖ).[^cite_note-afdfpoe-115] On 8 March 2016, the bureau of the ECR group began motions to exclude AfD from their group due to its links with the far-right FPÖ,[^cite_note-Euractiv1-116] inviting the two remaining AfD MEPs to leave the group by 31 March, with a motion of exclusion to be tabled on 12 April if they refuse to leave voluntarily.[^cite_note-EUobserver_March_2016-117] While von Storch left the ECR group on 8 April to join the [Europe of Freedom and Direct Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europe_of_Freedom_and_Direct_Democracy) group,[^cite_note-Storch_quits_ECR-118][^cite_note-Storch_joins_EFDD-119] Pretzell let himself be expelled on 12 April.[^cite_note-Pretzell_expelled-120]

With the [European migrant crisis](46415102.md) remaining the dominant national issue, elections on 13 March were held in the three states of [Baden-Württemberg](66401.md), [Rhineland-Palatinate](26239.md), and [Saxony-Anhalt](38138.md), and saw the AfD receiving double-digit percentages of the vote in all three states.[^cite_note-121][^cite_note-122] In the [2016 Saxony-Anhalt state election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Saxony-Anhalt_state_election), AfD reached second place in the Landtag, receiving 24.2% of the vote. In the [2016 Baden-Württemberg state election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Baden-W%C3%BCrttemberg_state_election), AfD achieved third place, with 15.1% of the vote. In the [2016 Rhineland-Palatinate state election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Rhineland-Palatinate_state_election), AfD again reached third place, with 12.6% of the vote. In [Angela Merkel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angela_Merkel)'s home state of [Mecklenburg-Vorpommern](38168.md), her CDU was beaten into third place following a strong showing of AfD, who contested at state level for the first time, to claim the second-highest polling with 20.8% of the vote in the [2016 state election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Mecklenburg-Vorpommern_state_election).[^cite_note-123] AfD voter support in Mecklenburg-Western Pomerania appears to have come from both left-wing and right-wing parties, with support for the [Social Democratic Party of Germany](60585.md) down 4.9%, CDU down 4.1%, [The Left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Left_\(Germany\)) down 5.2%, [Alliance '90/The Greens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alliance_'90/The_Greens) down 3.9%, and support for the [National Democratic Party of Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democratic_Party_of_Germany) (NDP) halved, dropping to 3.0%. Rising support for AfD meant that The Greens and the NDP failed to reach the 5% threshold to qualify for seats in the [Landtag of Mecklenburg-Vorpommern](17706695.md), and consequently lost their seats. In the [2016 Berlin state election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Berlin_state_election), which AfD also contested for the first time, the party achieved a vote of 14.2%, making them the fifth largest party represented in the state assembly. Their vote seems to have come equally from the SPD and CDU, whose votes declined 6.7% and 5.7%, respectively.[^cite_note-124]

At the party congress held on 30 April to 1 May 2016, AfD adopted a policy platform based upon opposition to Islam, calling for the ban of Islamic symbols including [burqas](165826.md), [minarets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minaret), and *[adhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adhan)* (call to prayer), using the slogan "Islam is not a part of Germany".[^cite_note-MM1-125][^cite_note-MM2-126][^cite_note-MM3-127][^cite_note-128]

### Meuthen's leadership (2017–2022)

Further information: [2017 German federal election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_German_federal_election) and [2021 German federal election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_German_federal_election)



Second vote share percentage for AfD in the 2017 federal election in Germany, final results



National party convention in [Cologne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cologne) in April 2017

At the party conference in April 2017, Frauke Petry announced that she would not run as the party's main candidate for the 2017 federal election. This announcement grew out of internal power struggle as the party's support had fallen in polls from 15% in the summer of 2016 to 7% just before the conference. [Björn Höcke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bj%C3%B6rn_H%C3%B6cke) from the far-right wing of the party and Petry were attempting to push each other out of the party. Petry's decision was partly seen as a step to avoid a vote at the conference on the issue of her standing.[^cite_note-129] The party chose Alexander Gauland, a stark conservative who worked as an editor and was a former member of the CDU,[^cite_note-130] to lead the party in the elections. Gauland supported the retention of Höcke's party membership. Alice Weidel, who is perceived as more moderate and [neoliberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoliberal), was elected as his running mate.[^cite_note-WSJ2017-04-131] The party approved a platform that, according to *[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)*, "urges Germany to close its borders to asylum applicants, end [sanctions on Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_sanctions_during_the_Ukrainian_crisis) and to leave the EU if Berlin fails to retrieve national sovereignty from Brussels, as well as to amend the country's constitution to allow people born to non-German parents to have their German citizenship revoked if they commit serious crimes."[^cite_note-WSJ2017-04-131]

In the 2017 federal election AfD won 12.6% of the vote and received 94 seats; this was the first time it had won seats in the [Bundestag](3768.md).[^cite_note-GB2017results-132][^cite_note-Wahlrecht2017-133] It won three constituency seats, which would have been enough to qualify for proportionally-elected seats in any event. Under a long-standing law intended to benefit regional parties, any party that wins at least three constituency seats qualifies for its share of proportionally-elected seats, regardless of vote share.[^cite_note-134]

At a press conference held by AfD the day after the 2017 federal election, Frauke Petry said that she would participate in the Bundestag as an independent; she said she did this because extremist statements by some members made it impossible for AfD to function as a constructive opposition, and to make clear to voters that there is internal dissent in the AfD. She also said that she would be leaving the party at some future date.[^cite_note-DW2017-09-135][^cite_note-136] Petry formed the [Blue Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Party_\(Germany\)) in September 2017. Four members of AfD in the [Mecklenburg-Western Pomerania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mecklenburg-Western_Pomerania) legislature, including Bernhard Wild, also left the party to form [Citizens for Mecklenburg-Vorpommern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizens_for_Mecklenburg-Vorpommern),[^cite_note-DW2017-09-135] which folded in December 2018. On 6 November 2019 Petry announced that the Blue Party would dissolve by the end of the year.[^cite_note-137]

In 2018 [André Poggenburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_Poggenburg), AfD's regional leader of the eastern [Saxony-Anhalt](38138.md) state, resigned his post after making racist remarks concerning Turks and immigrants with dual citizenship.[^cite_note-138][^cite_note-139] In 2019, Poggenburg started a new far-right party, *[Aufbruch deutscher Patrioten – Mitteldeutschland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aufbruch_deutscher_Patrioten_%E2%80%93_Mitteldeutschland)* (ADPM), which he left in August 2019 after his internal call to dissolve ADPM and to support AfD in the upcoming state elections of fall 2019 was denied.[^cite_note-140]

Ahead of the 2021 federal election, AfD campaigned with the slogan "Germany. But Normal" and took a position of opposing further lockdowns in response to the [COVID-19 pandemic in Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Germany). Having moved further right on economic issues and remaining strongly right on socio-cultural issues, despite attempts to normalize, AfD's manifesto for the federal election was deemed to be still too radical for the party to take part in government.[^cite_note-141]

In the federal election, AfD saw a dip in national vote share by getting 10.3% of the vote, compared to 12.6% in 2017; however, the party emerged as the largest in the states of [Saxony](28395.md) and [Thuringia](31130.md), and saw a strong performance in [eastern Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Germany).[^cite_note-142] The party's results drew a mixed analysis from AfD members and political commentators, the latter of whom attributed the slight decline to visible infighting, whereas AfD candidates such as Alice Weidel blamed media bias against the party. The political scientist [Kai Arzheimer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kai_Arzheimer) commented that the result "wasn't any appreciable increase, but it wasn't a disaster for them." Arzheimer also posited that the result demonstrated that AfD had firmly established itself in German national politics but had not reached beyond its core support. AfD's top candidates [Tino Chrupalla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tino_Chrupalla) and Weidel praised the result as "solid", while party spokesman [Jörg Meuthen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%B6rg_Meuthen) stated that the party should reevaluate the result and aim on "sending strong signals towards the center" to win back new voters.[^cite_note-Schultheis_2021-27] Meuthen left the party in January 2022.[^cite_note-143][^cite_note-144]

### Chrupalla and Weidel (2022–present)

AfD held their three seats in the [2022 Saarland state election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Saarland_state_election).[^cite_note-145] At the same time, they lost all their seats in the [2022 Schleswig-Holstein state election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Schleswig-Holstein_state_election).[^cite_note-146] In the [2022 Lower Saxony state election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Lower_Saxony_state_election) in October, AfD won 9 more seats compared to 2017 to a total of 18.[^cite_note-147] In the [2023 Berlin repeat state election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Berlin_repeat_state_election) AfD recorded a small upswing by gaining 4 seats compared to the 2021 election.[^cite_note-148] In the [2023 Bremen state election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Bremen_state_election) AfD did not participate and lost all their seats, as the Bremen electoral committee had barred AfD from the election due to internal divisions that had resulted in them submitting two lists of candidates. The [Citizens in Rage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizens_in_Rage), another right-wing party, participated instead; they received 10 seats (after having only one seat in 2019) in Bremen's state parliament.[^cite_note-149][^cite_note-150] On 25 June 2023, amidst rises in polls,[^cite_note-151] [Thuringia](31130.md)'s AfD won its first district election in [Sonneberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sonneberg_\(district\)).[^cite_note-152][^cite_note-153][^cite_note-154] In the [run-off election](25932.md) held on 2 July, the AfD candidate [Hannes Loth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hannes_Loth) won against the [independent politician](2172378.md) Nils Naumann,[^cite_note-155][^cite_note-156] becoming AfD's first ever mayor.[^cite_note-157][^cite_note-158][^cite_note-159]

On 8 October state elections, AfD significantly increased its share in [Hesse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Hessian_state_election) where it became the second-biggest party (+9 seats) and in [Bavaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Bavarian_state_election), where it became the third (+10 seats).

Observers considered the increase of support for the AfD as not being limited to the local level. [Opinion polling for the 2025 German federal election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opinion_polling_for_the_2025_German_federal_election) conducted in early July 2023 showed that the AfD polled more than the SPD, achieving second place behind the [CDU/CSU](10796483.md) alliance.[^cite_note-auto-160] The SPD co-leader said a ban should be considered if the AfD is categorized as a group of "proven Right-wing extremists" by the [Federal Office for the Protection of the Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Office_for_the_Protection_of_the_Constitution). [Friedrich Merz](1967626.md), the CDU leader, warned that "banning parties has never actually solved political problems". Germans are evenly split on a ban, with 47 per cent in favour and 47 per cent against; the ban is more popular in the west and among liberal Greens.[^cite_note-161]

In December 2023 Tim Lochner of AfD was elected Mayor of [Pirna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pirna) (Saxony). He became the first mayor of a city with more than 20,000 inhabitants to be a member of the party.[^cite_note-162]

In 2023, the AfD saw 86 [violent attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_violence) on AfD party representatives. This was more than on any other German party.[^cite_note-163][^cite_note-164]

#### 2023 meeting and subsequent protests

Main articles: [2023 Potsdam far-right meeting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Potsdam_far-right_meeting) and [2024–2025 German anti-extremism protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024%E2%80%932025_German_anti-extremism_protests)

In January 2024 [Correctiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Correctiv) reported that members of AfD had [secretly met](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meeting_of_right-wing_extremists_at_Potsdam_in_2023) with figures from the German and Austrian far-right in November 2023, in which they allegedly discussed a "[remigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remigration)" plan for deporting immigrants, which could include naturalised German citizens. Those present included the [Identitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Identitarian_movement) activist [Martin Sellner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Sellner).[^cite_note-165][^cite_note-166][^cite_note-\:3-167]

The AfD distanced itself from the meeting, saying it was not responsible for what was discussed and that its members had attended only in a personal capacity. Alice Weidel parted ways with [Roland Hartwig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roland_Hartwig), an advisor who was present at the meeting.[^cite_note-\:3-167][^cite_note-\:4-168]

The plan was condemned by German politicians, including Chancellor [Olaf Scholz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olaf_Scholz).[^cite_note-169][^cite_note-\:5-170] The report sparked [protests against the AfD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_German_anti-extremism_protests) across Germany, with protestors calling for a ban of AfD.[^cite_note-\:5-170][^cite_note-\:4-168][^cite_note-171][^cite_note-172] Subsequently, the AfD member [Maximilian Krah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximilian_Krah) got involved in controversy, prompting AfD to be expelled from the [ID group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Identity_and_Democracy), with [EKRE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_People's_Party_of_Estonia) supporting expulsion of Krah, but opposing the removal of the entire AfD delegation, and the [FPÖ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Party_of_Austria) opposing the expulsion of AfD.[^cite_note-173][^cite_note-174]

#### 2024

Further information: [2024 European Parliament election in Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_European_Parliament_election_in_Germany)



AfD in the [2024 European Parliament election in Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_European_Parliament_election_in_Germany)

On 9 June 2024 AfD won 16% of the vote in the European Parliament elections, second only to the [CDU/CSU](10796483.md) and almost five percentage points more than in the 2019 election.[^cite_note-175][^cite_note-\:6-176] AfD prevailed in all five former [East German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Germany) states.[^cite_note-177][^cite_note-178]

One of the party's leaders, [Tino Chrupalla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tino_Chrupalla), hailed the results as "historic".[^cite_note-\:6-176] In an attempt to rejoin the ID group, the AfD replaced its controversial candidate [Maximilian Krah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximilian_Krah) with [René Aust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ren%C3%A9_Aust) as head of the AfD delegation in the European Parliament.[^cite_note-euronews.com-179] However, AfD failed to join ID, or now named [Patriots for Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriots_for_Europe). Instead, AfD formed the new [ESN group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europe_of_Sovereign_Nations_Group) which was composed predominantly of AfD members, as well as some other [ethnonationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_nationalism) parties across Europe.[^cite_note-180]

#### State elections in the former East German states

In the [2024 Thuringian state election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Thuringian_state_election), the AfD became the first far-right party in Germany since the [Nazi Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_Party) to win a plurality of seats in a state election.[^cite_note-181][^cite_note-182] The AfD also performed strongly in [Brandenburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Brandenburg_state_election) and [Saxony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Saxony_state_election).[^cite_note-183]

#### 2025

Further information: [2025 German federal election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_German_federal_election)



Seating chart of the results

On 23 February 2025 AfD won 20.8% of the vote in the German federal election, second to the [CDU/CSU](10796483.md), and won 152 of 630 seats. It gained 10.4 percentage points over the 2021 election result.[^cite_note-184] The party won nearly a third of the votes in each of the East German states, the lowest being 32.5% in Brandenburg and the highest 38.5% in Thuringia, and won all but three constituency seats in the former East Germany (Potsdam, Erfurt and one of two seats in Leipzig). In the former West Germany, the party beat the SPD for second place by less than 0.5% of the vote and received more than 20% of the vote in two southwestern states: [Saarland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saarland) (21.6%) and [Rhineland-Palatinate](26239.md) (20.1%).[^cite_note-185]

In April 2025, for the first time in German history, AfD was ahead of the CDU/CSU (Union) in some [opinion polls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opinion_poll) for the [next federal election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Next_German_federal_election), while other polls put it on par with the Union or in second place. Support for the CDU/CSU, an [alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_alliance) that won the election, fell from 29% to 24%, while AfD rose by three percentage points from 22% to 25%, partially becoming the most popular party in Germany.[^cite_note-186][^cite_note-187]

On 2 May 2025 the [Federal Office for the Protection of the Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Office_for_the_Protection_of_the_Constitution) (BfV) classified AfD as a "confirmed right-wing extremist endeavor".[^cite_note-\:10-66][^cite_note-provenextremist-67] In a 1,100-page classified and undisclosed report, BfV experts found that AfD is a "racist and [anti-Muslim](162173.md) organisation". The classification will allow German authorities to monitor AfD and possibly limit or halt [public funding](146719.md) for it. It may also inspire its opponents to attempt to get it banned.[^cite_note-Reuters020525-188][^cite_note-\:11-189] AfD leaders [Alice Weidel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alice_Weidel) and [Tino Chrupalla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tino_Chrupalla) said the decision was "clearly politically motivated" and was a "severe blow to German democracy". They argued that AfD is being discredited and [persecuted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution) by the government.[^cite_note-BBCNews-19] Chancellor [Olaf Scholz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olaf_Scholz) said that BfV provided a "very detailed justification" for its decision and that proceedings to ban the AfD must not be rushed.[^cite_note-\:11-189] Several [federal states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_states) in Germany have begun considering measures that would ban AfD members from being employed in jobs like [civil servants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_service), [police officers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_officer), [teachers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teacher) or [soldiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soldier) in response to the classification.[^cite_note-190]

On 4 May 2025 a poll conducted by Insa Polling Institute which surveyed 1,001 people found that 48% of them favour banning AfD. The poll also showed that 61% of participants consider AfD a "right-wing extremist" party.[^cite_note-191] On 5 May AfD sued the BfV, accusing it of violating the [Basic Law for the Federal Republic of Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basic_Law_for_the_Federal_Republic_of_Germany) (the German [constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution)) by trying to prosecute AfD for saying ideas which are considered [freedom of speech](21401843.md) and legitimate criticism of German [immigration](4599312.md) policies.[^cite_note-192][^cite_note-193] A court in [Cologne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cologne) will start reviewing the case once BfV confirms that it has been notified of the lawsuit.[^cite_note-194]

[Sieghard Knodel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sieghard_Knodel), a member of AfD in the [Bundestag](3768.md), announced his resignation from the party on 6 May 2025. He wrote in an email: "In light of the classification of the party as confirmed right-wing extremist by the Federal Office for the Protection of the Constitution, I must protect my private and business environment". He said that he would continue to serve as a non-attached member of parliament.[^cite_note-195]

On May 8, the extremist classification of AfD was temporarily suspended by the BfV pending an official [court judgement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judgment_\(law\)).[^cite_note-196][^cite_note-197] The AfD's leaders said that the decision was a "first important step" that would help "counter the accusation of [right-wing extremism](221220.md)".[^cite_note-198] The report by the BfV that led to the classification was later leaked to the public.[^cite_note-\:14-199][^cite_note-\:13-200]

For the [Ludwigshafen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ludwigshafen) mayoral election in September 2025, the local election administration commission composed of members of other parties, decided that AfD candidate [Joachim Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joachim_Paul) would not appear on the ballot due to doubt about his support for the [constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grundgesetz), a move which was later upheld in court, despite Joachim Paul never having had his right to be a candidate in elections revoked by law or any court.[^cite_note-201] In August, five direct and two reserve AfD candidates died within 14 days of each other ahead of [local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_North_Rhine-Westphalia_local_elections) in [North Rhine-Westphalia](38414.md).[^cite_note-202][^cite_note-203]

#### 2026

In 2026, the AfD received its best ever result in a state election, winning 43.8% of the vote in the [2026 Saxony-Anhalt state election](70945225.md). The party barely missed out on an absolute majority, winning 39 seats while 42 were required for a majority.[^cite_note-204]
